| `first=-1&max=5` | 200 | 5 roles: `admin`, `create-realm`, `default-roles-master`, `gloak-pg-i`, `gloak-pg-j` |
| `first=1&max=-1` | 200 | 22 roles, sorted by name, `admin` dropped |
| `first=1&max=0` | 200 | `[]` |
| `first=99&max=5` | 200 | `[]` |
| `search=&max=2` | 200 | all 23, in the same unsorted order as the `(none)` row |
| `search=&first=1&max=5` | 200 | 5 roles: `create-realm`, `default-roles-master`, `gloak-pg-i`, `gloak-pg-j`, `gloak-pg-k` |

The decisive row's body, verbatim:

```
GET /admin/realms/master/roles?first=1&max=5
200
[{"id":"fa09069c-9c9d-4b5e-bc18-c880a96176b7","name":"create-realm","description":"${role_create-realm}","composite":false,"clientRole":false,"containerId":"0c3e15df-0100-431c-a4a3-7a3d1630b20e"},{"id":"d77a1ec5-7ad8-4f60-a785-896e6e12d534","name":"default-roles-master","description":"${role_default-roles}","composite":true,"clientRole":false,"containerId":"0c3e15df-0100-431c-a4a3-7a3d1630b20e"},{"id":"84545650-deb7-4842-8b30-5b8ccfbe18ef","name":"gloak-pg-i","composite":false,"clientRole":false,"containerId":"0c3e15df-0100-431c-a4a3-7a3d1630b20e"},{"id":"24abbccd-52f3-48e0-97c1-ce47971e6fa2","name":"gloak-pg-j","composite":false,"clientRole":false,"containerId":"0c3e15df-0100-431c-a4a3-7a3d1630b20e"},{"id":"8a020959-966b-432c-a979-2dea2435fd3d","name":"gloak-pg-k","composite":false,"clientRole":false,"containerId":"0c3e15df-0100-431c-a4a3-7a3d1630b20e"}]
```

Five roles, not 23. `first` and `max` page with no `search` term anywhere in
the request.

The same queries with `search=gloak-pg-`, which matches all eighteen:

| Query | Status | Body |
|---|---|---|
| `search=gloak-pg-` | 200 | all 18, sorted by name: `gloak-pg-i` … `gloak-pg-z` |
| `search=gloak-pg-&max=5` | 200 | 5 roles: `gloak-pg-i`, `gloak-pg-j`, `gloak-pg-k`, `gloak-pg-l`, `gloak-pg-m` |
| `search=gloak-pg-&first=1` | 200 | 17 roles, `gloak-pg-j` … `gloak-pg-z` |
| `search=gloak-pg-&first=1&max=5` | 200 | 5 roles: `gloak-pg-j`, `gloak-pg-k`, `gloak-pg-l`, `gloak-pg-m`, `gloak-pg-n` |

And `GET /admin/realms/master/clients/{gloak-pg-client uuid}/roles`, which
agrees with the realm listing row for row:

| Query | Status | Body |
|---|---|---|
| (none) | 200 | all 18, order `gloak-pg-o`, `gloak-pg-p`, `gloak-pg-q`, `gloak-pg-u`, `gloak-pg-t`, `gloak-pg-k`, `gloak-pg-z`, `gloak-pg-r`, `gloak-pg-m`, `gloak-pg-w`, `gloak-pg-y`, `gloak-pg-x`, `gloak-pg-i`, `gloak-pg-l`, `gloak-pg-j`, `gloak-pg-n`, `gloak-pg-s`, `gloak-pg-v` |
| `first=1&max=5` | 200 | 5 roles: `gloak-pg-j`, `gloak-pg-k`, `gloak-pg-l`, `gloak-pg-m`, `gloak-pg-n` |
| `first=5&max=5` | 200 | 5 roles: `gloak-pg-n`, `gloak-pg-o`, `gloak-pg-p`, `gloak-pg-q`, `gloak-pg-r` |
| `first=0&max=5` | 200 | 5 roles: `gloak-pg-i`, `gloak-pg-j`, `gloak-pg-k`, `gloak-pg-l`, `gloak-pg-m` |
| `max=5` | 200 | all 18, in the same unsorted order as the `(none)` row |
| `first=1` | 200 | all 18, in the same unsorted order as the `(none)` row |
| `first=-1&max=-1` | 200 | all 18, sorted by name |
| `search=&max=5` | 200 | all 18, in the same unsorted order as the `(none)` row |
| `first=1&max=5&search=gloak-pg-` | 200 | 5 roles: `gloak-pg-j`, `gloak-pg-k`, `gloak-pg-l`, `gloak-pg-m`, `gloak-pg-n` |

#### What the rows show

**Both bounds present is a second gate, independent of `search`.** `max=5`
alone and `first=1` alone return all 23; `first=1&max=5` returns five. The
only difference between them is that the second sends both.

**The paged path is sorted by name; the unpaginated path is not sorted at
all.** The eighteen roles were created `z` first and `i` last, so creation
order is the reverse of alphabetical order. Every paged row comes back
alphabetical - `first=0&max=5` is `admin`, `create-realm`,
`default-roles-master`, `gloak-pg-i`, `gloak-pg-j`, and `search=gloak-pg-`
alone is `gloak-pg-i` … `gloak-pg-z`, not `gloak-pg-z` … `gloak-pg-i`. The
`(none)`, `max=5` and `first=1` rows come back in the unstable order the
"realm role listing is not sorted" section above records. This also corrects
the older claim, below, that a `search` result comes back in creation order:
it does not, and the `-a`/`-b`/`-c` probe set could not have told the
difference.

**This explains the order difference the older rows recorded and could not
account for.** `first=-1&max=-1` came back sorted while `max=2` and `first=1`
came back unsorted. Under a "search only" rule all three take the identical
path and must be byte-identical, which they were not. Under the measured rule
they do not: `first=-1&max=-1` has both bounds present, so it takes the paged,
name-sorted path, where a negative bound then means "no bound" rather than "do
not page". `max=2` and `first=1` send one bound each and take the unpaginated
path. `first=-1&max=5` and `first=1&max=-1` confirm the sign is irrelevant to
the gate: both page, and both are sorted.

**An empty `search` is not a non-empty one.** `search=&max=2` returns all 23
unsorted, exactly as `max=2` does, while `search=&first=1&max=5` returns the
same five roles as `first=1&max=5`. The empty value neither opens the gate nor
closes it; only the two bounds matter on those rows.

**`max` is a page size, `first` a zero-based offset**, both counted over the
sorted order. `first=0&max=5` takes the first five, `first=5&max=5` takes the
five starting at index 5, `max=0` is an empty array rather than everything, and
a `first` past the end (`first=99` over 23 roles) is `[]`, not an error.

#### Upstream agrees; the earlier claim of a contradiction was wrong

`RealmRolesSearchTest.testPaginationRoles` creates 15 roles and asserts
`list(1, 5).size() == 5`, `list(5, 5).size() == 5`, and
`list(1, null).size() > 15`. All three hold against the measurement above:
`first=1&max=5` and `first=5&max=5` each return five, and `first=1` with `max`
omitted returns the whole set.

The earlier version of this section reported a contradiction with
`testPaginationRoles`. There is none. It compared a measurement of
`list(1, null)` - `first=1` alone, which upstream itself asserts does *not*
page - against upstream's assertion about `list(1, 5)`, a query nobody had
issued. Two different calls, and the one that was run agreed with upstream
rather than contradicting it.

#### The earlier 2026-08-25 rows

Measured 2026-08-25 against quay.io/keycloak/keycloak:26.7.1, covering both
role listings. The realm listing, `GET /admin/realms/master/roles`, was
narrowed with `search=gloak-probe-page` to three roles created for the
purpose, then again with `search=gloak-probe-neg` to a second set of three,
to check negative bounds. The client listing,
`GET /admin/realms/master/clients/{uuid}/roles`, was narrowed with
`search=gloak-probe-cr` to three roles on a client created for the purpose
(`gloak-probe-clientpage`), to check whether it pages under the same rule.

These rows are all still accurate as bytes. Two of their **descriptions** are
corrected by the 2026-08-26 probes above: "in that order" means alphabetical
order, not creation order, because each probe set was created `-a`, `-b`, `-c`
and so cannot distinguish the two; and the no-`search` rows show what one
bound does, not what `first` and `max` do.

| Query | Status | Body |
|---|---|---|
| `search=gloak-probe-page` | 200 | 3 roles: `gloak-probe-page-a`, `gloak-probe-page-b`, `gloak-probe-page-c`, in that order |
| `search=gloak-probe-page&max=0` | 200 | `[]` |
| `search=gloak-probe-page&max=2` | 200 | 2 roles: `gloak-probe-page-a`, `gloak-probe-page-b` |
| `search=gloak-probe-page&first=1` | 200 | 2 roles: `gloak-probe-page-b`, `gloak-probe-page-c` |
| `search=gloak-probe-page&first=3` | 200 | `[]` |
| `search=gloak-probe-page&first=1&max=1` | 200 | 1 role: `gloak-probe-page-b` |
| `max=2` (no `search`) | 200 | all 8 realm roles, unpaginated - abbreviated to names and count: `admin`, `create-realm`, `default-roles-master`, `offline_access`, `uma_authorization`, the three probe roles |
| `first=1` (no `search`) | 200 | same 8 roles as the row above, same order |
| `search=&max=2` (`search` present but empty) | 200 | same 8 roles, unpaginated |
| `first=-1&max=-1` | 200 | same 8 roles, sorted alphabetically by name |
| `search=gloak-probe-neg&first=-1&max=-1` | 200 | 3 roles: `gloak-probe-neg-a`, `gloak-probe-neg-b`, `gloak-probe-neg-c`, in that order |
| `search=gloak-probe-neg&max=-1` | 200 | same 3 roles, same order |
| `search=gloak-probe-neg&first=-1` | 200 | same 3 roles, same order |
| `search=gloak-probe-neg&first=0&max=-1` | 200 | same 3 roles, same order |
| `search=gloak-probe-cr` (client listing) | 200 | 3 roles: `gloak-probe-cr-a`, `gloak-probe-cr-b`, `gloak-probe-cr-c`, in that order |
| `search=gloak-probe-cr&max=2` (client listing) | 200 | 2 roles: `gloak-probe-cr-a`, `gloak-probe-cr-b` |
| `search=gloak-probe-cr&first=1` (client listing) | 200 | 2 roles: `gloak-probe-cr-b`, `gloak-probe-cr-c` |
| `search=gloak-probe-cr&max=0` (client listing) | 200 | `[]` |
| `max=2` (client listing, no `search`) | 200 | all 3 client roles, unpaginated, order `gloak-probe-cr-b`, `gloak-probe-cr-a`, `gloak-probe-cr-c` |
| `first=1` (client listing, no `search`) | 200 | same 3 roles, same order as the row above |

`max` is a page size and `first` a zero-based offset. `max=2` keeps the first
two; `first=1` drops the first and keeps the rest; the two together compose to
the single role in between. `max=0` is an empty array, not everything. `first`
past the end of the matched set (`first=3` over three matches) is `[]`, not an
error. The order they are counted over is alphabetical, which the 2026-08-26
probes establish and these rows cannot: `-a`, `-b`, `-c` was both the creation
sequence and the alphabetical one.

The no-`search` rows here - `max=2`, `first=1` and `search=&max=2` - each send
at most one bound, so all three take the unpaginated path and return
everything. That is consistent with the rule at the top of this section, and
it is all these three rows show. `first=-1&max=-1` sends both, so it takes the
paged path, which is why it alone came back sorted.

A negative `first` or `max` behaves as absent: all four `gloak-probe-neg`
queries in the table return the full three-role match, unbounded. A negative
bound is not treated as a real offset or page size, with `search` or without
it - but, as the 2026-08-26 rows show, it still counts as *present* for the
gate.

**The client role listing pages under the same rule as the realm one.**
Probed once, on `GET /admin/realms/master/clients/{uuid}/roles`:
`search=gloak-probe-cr` returns all three client roles; `max=2` keeps the
first two, `first=1` drops the first, and `max=0` is `[]` - the same shape the
realm listing's `search=gloak-probe-page` rows show. With no `search`, `max=2`
and `first=1` both come back with all three client roles, unpaginated, in the
same unsorted order as each other, matching the class of order instability
this document records for the realm listing's own plain listing. The
2026-08-26 client rows above extend this to the both-bounds case and to
eighteen roles, and the two listings agree there too.

Reproducibility across container starts: agrees for the narrowed
queries. `search=gloak-probe-page&max=2` and
`search=gloak-probe-page&first=1&max=1` returned the same role names in the
same order both times. It does not agree for the unfiltered `max=2` and
`first=1` order, which differed between the two runs while matching each
other within a run - the same instability the "not sorted" section above
records for the plain listing, since neither of them actually pages. The
2026-08-26 run is a third container start and agrees with both on the queries
they share.

Mined from `tests/base/src/test/java/org/keycloak/tests/admin/realm/RealmRolesSearchTest.java`,
methods `testSearchForRoles` and `testPaginationRoles`, at Keycloak 26.7.1.

### Writing a role

| Request | Status | Body |
|---|---|---|
| `POST /roles` | 201 | empty, `Location` and `content-length: 0` |
| `POST` a name that exists | 409 | `{"errorMessage":"Role with name probe-role already exists"}` |
| `POST` with no name | 400 | `{"error":"role has no name"}` |
| `GET`/`PUT`/`DELETE` a missing role | 404 | `{"error":"Could not find role"}` |
| `PUT` with no name | 400 | `{"error":"role has no name"}` |
| `PUT` | 204 | empty |
| `DELETE` | 204 | empty, `Cache-Control: no-cache` |

**`Location` names the role by name, not by id**:
`http://host/admin/realms/master/roles/probe-role`. The client and user creates
both put a UUID there.

Three of those bodies are three different error families on one endpoint: the
RFC-ish `{"errorMessage"}` for the conflict, the bare `{"error"}` carrying
lowercase prose for the missing name, and the bare `{"error"}` carrying
sentence case for the missing role.

### `PUT` on a role replaces; `PUT` on a client or a user merges

Measured directly. A role carrying `description` "a probe", updated with
`{"name":"probe-renamed"}` alone, comes back **with no description**. The
client and user updates next door unmarshal the body over the current
representation and keep what the caller omitted.

**A role can be renamed.** `PUT` with a new `name` answers 204, the id survives,
and the old path 404s. A user's username, by contrast, is immutable through its
own `PUT`.

**`composite` in the `PUT` body is ignored, not applied.** A role with no
children, `PUT` with `{"name":"...","composite":true}`, answers 204 and reads
back `composite:false` with no children - the field is server-computed from
the role's own children (see "The parent's own `composite` flag is derived,
not latched" below) and a write cannot set it directly. Gloak already does
this: `applyRoleUpdate` copies `current.Composite` onto the updated role and
never reads `rep.Composite` from the body, so this matches.

### Composites

`POST` and `DELETE` on `.../composites` both take an **array of role
representations** in the body and answer 204. `id` is what they act on.

`GET .../composites` returns realm and client children mixed, in the brief
serialisation. `.../composites/realm` and
`.../composites/clients/{targetClientUuid}` are filters over the same list.
**The brief serialisation holds regardless of `briefRepresentation`** -
`?briefRepresentation=false` on `GET /roles/admin/composites` was checked
directly and still comes back with six keys and no `attributes`, unlike the
plain role listing next door where the same parameter adds it.

**The parent's own `composite` flag is derived, not latched.** It flips to
`true` when the first child is added and back to `false` when the last one is
removed, both unprompted, measured on the same role in the same session:
`probe-parent-realm` read `composite:true` once `probe-child-realm` was added
and `composite:false` again once that same child was removed. The rule is
"has at least one composite child", checked fresh, not a flag that only ever
moves one way. Neither store driver recomputes this on
`AddComposite`/`RemoveComposite`, so the handler does: after every
`POST`/`DELETE .../composites`, it re-lists the role's children and writes
the flag to match whether any remain, rather than setting it unconditionally
on add.

**`POST .../composites` validates the whole batch before applying any of
it.** A body with one valid role id and one that does not exist - tried both
orders, valid-first and invalid-first - answers 404
`{"error":"Could not find composite role"}` and leaves the role with **no**
children at all, not the valid one alone. That is an eighth spelling of
not-found (see the tally below) and it is specific to this endpoint - it is
not `writeRoleNotFound`'s "Could not find role".

`eachComposite` in `internal/admin/roles.go` now matches this: it validates
every id in the batch in a first pass before applying any of them in a
second, so a bad entry anywhere in the body leaves the role's children
untouched and answers `writeCompositeRoleNotFound`'s
`{"error":"Could not find composite role"}`, not `writeRoleNotFound`'s
"Could not find role". `DELETE .../composites` shares the same function and
was kept consistent with `POST` on this, absent a measurement showing the two
differ - removing a child that is not attached is still a no-op 204, since
that id resolves to a real role and only fails the *attached* check inside
`RemoveComposite`, not the existence check above it.

### `/roles/{name}/users` is direct holders only

Not transitive. The administrator holds `admin`, which is composite over
`create-realm`; `GET /roles/admin/users` returns the administrator and
`GET /roles/create-realm/users` returns `[]`.

The user representation there carries **no `access` block** - a fourth
serialisation of a user, matching the service-account one.

`/roles/{name}/groups` answers `[]` on a realm with no groups.

### `roles-by-id` is the same resource by a different key

Same representation, same composite sub-resources. Its 404 is a **different
message**: `{"error":"Could not find role with id"}`.

**Resolving the role happens before the caller is judged, so this 404 always
comes out ahead of any 403** the guard would otherwise answer: a missing role
id is 404 whatever the caller holds, and an existing one the caller may not
see is 403. That lets an unauthorized caller tell "does not exist" apart from
"exists, but I may not touch it" - exactly the ordering an access-control
design would normally avoid. It is Keycloak's own measured order, not a
defensive choice. An earlier version of this project's own plan reasoned it
the other way round, as if enforcing 404-first were what *avoided* the leak;
it is what causes it.

That makes eight spellings of not-found measured so far: `Could not find
client`, `User not found`, `Realm not found.` with its full stop, `Credential
not found`, `Could not find role`, `Role not found`, `Could not find role with
id`, `Could not find composite role`. A ninth arrived with the client
role-mapping reads: `Client not found`, which is the *mapping* routes' answer
for an unknown client UUID where the client and role endpoints send `Could not
find client` for the very same UUID. See "A client UUID that resolves to nothing
has its own not-found spelling" below.

### Which role each role operation needs

Measured by giving a user exactly one `master-realm` role and trying each
operation, the same method the client and user tables used, then re-measured
for Task 9 against the paired manage role too, because the first pass only
tried the view role of each pair and looked like a lone requirement.

| Operations | Read | Write |
|---|---|---|
| `/roles`, `/roles/{name}`, their composites and `.../groups` | `view-realm` **or** `manage-realm` | `manage-realm` of the role's own family - a composite write with a cross-family child needs more, see below |
| `/clients/{uuid}/roles/...` and its composites and `.../groups` | `view-clients` **or** `manage-clients` | `manage-clients`, same caveat |
| `/roles-by-id/{id}/...` | follows the resolved role's container: the same either/or pair, picked by the role's own family rather than by the route | the manage role of the role's own family, same caveat |
| `.../roles/{name}/users` (both forms) | **a conjunction, not an either/or**: one role from the row above, **and** one of `view-users`/`manage-users`/`query-users`, held together - neither family alone opens it, and two roles from the same family do not either | n/a, no write |

**"Read" means either role of the pair, not the view role alone**, checked
directly on the plain reads, the composite listings and `.../groups`, on both
realm roles and client roles - the tables further down carry the raw
pairwise results. The plan behind this cut assumed a single required role
four separate times, and the paired manage role opened the operation every
time it was actually tried.

**The write column above is the same-family case.** A composite write
(`POST`/`DELETE .../composites`) whose body carries a child from the *other*
family needs the child's own manage role too, and only on `POST` - `DELETE`
does not enforce it, in either direction; see the cross-family table below.
`POST .../composites` also validates every id in the batch before applying
any of them, and a batch mixing a bad id with a forbidden cross-family child
is decided by **array order**: whichever bad entry comes first is the error
returned. The `composite` flag itself is not part of either column - it is
derived from whether the role currently has children, recomputed after every
add and remove rather than stored.

`roles-by-id` is the interesting one: the guard is decided by the data, not by
the route. A client role reached by id needs `view-clients` or
`manage-clients`; a realm role reached by the same path needs `view-realm` or
`manage-realm` - the same view/manage pair the by-name reads take, just picked
by the resolved role's container instead of by the route. `view-users`,
`manage-users`, `query-clients` and `query-users` are refused everywhere in
this table - **except `.../users` below, where the first two of those are half
of what it takes.**

**Re-measured for Task 9, because the first pass only tried `view-realm` and
`view-clients`.** That looked like a lone required role each and was recorded
that way once already; trying `manage-realm` and `manage-clients` too, on both
a realm role id and a client role id, shows they open the read side exactly
as well as their `view-*` counterpart:

| Caller holds | `GET /roles-by-id/{a realm role}` | `GET /roles-by-id/{a client role}` |
|---|---|---|
| `view-realm` | 200 | 403 |
| `manage-realm` | 200 | 403 |
| `view-clients` | 403 | 200 |
| `manage-clients` | 403 | 200 |
| `view-users` | 403 | 403 |

The three composite reads (`.../composites`, `.../composites/realm`,
`.../composites/clients/{uuid}`) reached by id take the identical pair,
checked the same way rather than assumed from the plain read next to it.

**The write side is narrower, and single for `PUT`, `DELETE`, and for
`POST`/`DELETE .../composites` when every child in the body shares the
parent's own family: only the manage role on the resolved role's own side**,
matching the by-name writes exactly. (A composite write with a child from the
*other* family needs more than this - see the cross-family table below.)

| Caller holds | `PUT`/`DELETE` a realm role | `PUT`/`DELETE` a client role |
|---|---|---|
| `view-realm` | 403 | 403 |
| `manage-realm` | **204** | 403 |
| `view-clients` | 403 | 403 |
| `manage-clients` | 403 | **204** |

**`POST .../composites` needs a composite child's own manage role,
independent of the parent's - `DELETE .../composites` does not. A gap
`guardByRoleContainer` cannot close by itself, and an asymmetry between the
two verbs that nothing about the route explains.** `POST`/`DELETE
.../composites` reached by id both check the *parent's* container to decide
between `manage-realm` and `manage-clients` at the route-guard level, because
that is all the route-level guard can see before the body is decoded. On
`POST` that is not the whole story, measured directly against a live 26.7.1:
`manage-clients` alone adds a client-role child to a client-role parent
(204), but the identical caller adding a *realm*-role child to that same
parent is refused (403) and only succeeds once it also holds `manage-realm`.
The mirror holds on the realm side with a client-role child:

| Caller holds | client-role parent + client-role child | client-role parent + realm-role child | realm-role parent + realm-role child | realm-role parent + client-role child |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `manage-clients` alone | **204** | 403 | n/a | 403 |
| `manage-realm` alone | n/a | 403 | **204** | 403 |
| `manage-clients` + `manage-realm` | 204 | **204** | 204 | **204** |

All four cells above are `POST`. **`DELETE` does not carry this requirement
at all, in either direction** - the identical caller, holding only the
parent-side manage role, removes a cross-family child with a plain 204. See
"M6" under Task 10 below for the raw transcript; the two sections used to
read as if they contradicted each other (this one implied both verbs, M6
measured only `DELETE` and found the opposite) and have been reconciled
here rather than left for a reader to notice the gap. Gloak matches this now:
`eachComposite` in `internal/admin/roles.go` takes the per-child check as a
`checkChild` parameter, `addComposites` passes it and `removeComposites`
passes `nil`. Nobody knows why Keycloak enforces this on one verb and not the
other; it is implemented as measured, not rationalised.

**Confirmed the same on the by-name route, not assumed from `roles-by-id`.**
`POST /roles/{name}/composites` with a client-role child, caller holding
`manage-realm` alone:

```
POST /admin/realms/master/roles/byname-cross-family-parent/composites
Authorization: Bearer <manage-realm-only token>
Body: [{"id":"49d7c924-...","name":"byname-child-role"}]

HTTP/1.1 403 Forbidden
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
```

The same request from a caller holding `manage-realm` and `manage-clients`
together:

```
HTTP/1.1 204 No Content
```

Matches the `roles-by-id` table exactly. This matters because the fix lives
in `eachComposite`, which both route families share - the guard that decides
the *parent's* required role differs between them
(`guard("manage-realm", ...)` by name, `guardByRoleContainer` by id), so the
by-id result did not by itself guarantee the by-name route behaved the same
way; it does, and now it is measured rather than inferred.

`eachComposite` in `internal/admin/roles.go` closes this: after resolving
each child id (the same pass that answers the composite 404 above), it checks
the caller against that child's own container - `manage-realm` for a realm
child, `manage-clients` for a client child - in addition to, not instead of,
the route-level guard's check on the parent. That is the only place in the
request that has both the caller and every child resolved, since the
route-level guard runs before the body is decoded.

**"Read" admits either role of the pair, and this was checked separately for
the composite sub-resources rather than assumed from the plain reads next
door** - two earlier tables in this document (clients and client roles) needed
exactly that correction. Four single-role users -
`only-view-realm`/`only-manage-realm`/`only-view-clients`/`only-manage-clients`
- were each pointed at `GET /roles/admin/composites` and at
`GET /clients/{account uuid}/roles/manage-account/composites`:

| Caller holds | `GET /roles/{name}/composites` | `GET /clients/{uuid}/roles/{name}/composites` |
|---|---|---|
| `view-realm` | 200 | 403 |
| `manage-realm` | 200 | 403 |
| `view-clients` | 403 | 200 |
| `manage-clients` | 403 | 200 |

Composite reads are `guardAny(realmRolesReadRoles, ...)` / `guardAny(clientRolesReadRoles, ...)`
- the same two-role pairs as the plain reads, not the single role the
sub-resource's own name might suggest.

**Writes on `.../composites` (`POST` and `DELETE`) take only the manage role
on their own side, not the pair** - checked the same way, against
`probe-parent-realm`/`probe-parent-client`:

| Caller holds | `POST`/`DELETE /roles/{name}/composites` | `POST`/`DELETE /clients/{uuid}/roles/{name}/composites` |
|---|---|---|
| `view-realm` | 403 | 403 |
| `manage-realm` | **204** | 403 |
| `view-clients` | 403 | 403 |
| `manage-clients` | 403 | **204** |

**`.../groups` takes the plain view/manage pair, exactly like the composite
reads above it; `.../users` does not - it needs one role from *each* of two
families.** Checked with four single-role callers on `.../groups`, the same
method as the composite table above:

| Caller holds | `GET /roles/{name}/groups` | `GET /clients/{uuid}/roles/{name}/groups` |
|---|---|---|
| `view-realm` | 200 | 403 |
| `manage-realm` | 200 | 403 |
| `view-clients` | 403 | 200 |
| `manage-clients` | 403 | 200 |

`.../users` was checked the same way and none of those four roles - alone -
opens it, on either side:

| Caller holds | `GET /roles/{name}/users` | `GET /clients/{uuid}/roles/{name}/users` |
|---|---|---|
| `view-realm` | 403 | 403 |
| `manage-realm` | 403 | 403 |
| `view-clients` | 403 | 403 |
| `manage-clients` | 403 | 403 |
| `view-users` | 403 | 403 |
| `manage-users` | 403 | 403 |
| `query-users` | 403 | 403 |
| all seven together | 200 | 200 |

Bisecting the seven-role combination down: `view-realm`+`view-users` alone is
already 200 on the realm side, and `view-clients`+`view-users` alone is
already 200 on the client side - so it is a pair, not the full set, and it is
a **conjunction of two families**: one role from the same view/manage pair
`.../groups` takes, **and** one of `view-users`/`manage-users`/`query-users`,
together. Either half alone, including all four management roles held
together with no user-read role, is 403. Confirmed both ways round -
`manage-realm`+`query-users`, `manage-clients`+`manage-users` and others - and
confirmed that the pairing is side-specific: `view-clients`+`view-users` opens
the client route and not the realm one, and `view-realm`+`view-users` opens
the realm route and not the client one.

**"Any two roles from the relevant set" was checked and excluded, not just
argued against**: two roles from the *same* family, with nothing from the
other, were tried on both routes - `view-realm`+`manage-realm` alone (403 on
both) and `view-users`+`query-users` alone (403 on both). Two roles is not
sufficient by itself; it has to be one from each of the two named families.

**Correction to a previous version of this table.** The all-seven row
originally read `200 | 403` - 403 on the client route despite the caller
holding `view-clients`+`view-users`, a strict subset of those seven roles,
which already opens that same route on its own. That is not a result RBAC
produces (a superset cannot grant less than one of its own subsets), and it
had the signature of the token-expiry artifact described above: the original
measurement session fetched all seven role representations sequentially
before minting the probe user's token, on tokens that last 60 seconds.
Re-measured with role representations cached up front and a token minted
immediately before each call, exactly as prescribed for the token-expiry
problem: `200 | 200`, consistent with every pairwise result already in this
section. The table cell above now reads the corrected value; the old one -
`200 | 403` - is spelled out here rather than left to a diff, because a
reader who previously saw `403` needs to know it was wrong and why, not just
that the number changed.

Gloak's `guardAny(realmRolesReadRoles, ...)` / `guardAny(clientRolesReadRoles,
...)` from the plain reads cannot express a two-family conjunction, so
`.../users` is registered with a new combinator, `guardAnyAndAny`, built from
`guardAnyRejecting` rather than duplicated - see `internal/admin/router.go`.
The corrected measurement does not change this: `guardAnyAndAny` already
grants 200 to a caller holding all seven roles on both routes, since holding
all seven means both `hasAny` checks pass regardless of side.

**`POST /clients/{master-realm uuid}/roles` is 403 for a full administrator.**
The realm's own client will not take new roles from anybody, which is the same
rule as "the realm's own client is never configurable" in the client section.

**The same refusal extends to writing composites on a role the realm's own
client already has, not just to creating a new one.** Measured against a live
26.7.1 with the full administrator token, on **both** composite verbs,
re-measured 2026-08-25:

```
POST /admin/realms/master/clients/{master-realm uuid}/roles/query-groups/composites
Authorization: Bearer <full administrator token>
Body: [{"id":"...","name":"probe-child"}]

HTTP/1.1 403 Forbidden
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
```
```
DELETE /admin/realms/master/clients/{master-realm uuid}/roles/query-groups/composites
Authorization: Bearer <full administrator token>
Body: [{"id":"...","name":"probe-child"}]

HTTP/1.1 403 Forbidden
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
```
```
GET /admin/realms/master/clients/{master-realm uuid}/roles/query-groups/composites
Authorization: Bearer <full administrator token>

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
[]
```

Even the bootstrapped administrator, which passes every guard in this
document, is refused on the two writes - the same "realm's own client is never
configurable" rule, applied to an existing role's composites rather than to
creating one. The read is not refused, so the rule is on the writes alone.
The `DELETE` and the `GET` are new with this re-measurement; only the `POST`
had been recorded before, and the `DELETE` was not assumed from it - this
document already has one case (the cross-family child rule) where `POST` and
`DELETE` were measured and turned out to differ.

**Gloak matched this from 2026-08-25.** It had answered 204 to the identical
request, which is worse than a cosmetic divergence: a caller holding only
`manage-clients` could `POST` a composite onto `master-realm`'s own
`manage-clients` role naming **`manage-realm`** as the child, and every check
passed - the route guard wants `manage-clients`, and the per-child
`requiresChildManageRole` wants `manage-clients` too, because `manage-realm`
is itself a client role on that same client. `roles.Effective` then returned
`manage-realm` on the next request. Nothing outside could mint a narrow-role
admin at the time, so it was not reachable, but role assignment is the next
cut.

**The check is in `eachComposite`, not in `clientRoleContainer`.** An earlier
revision of this passage said the fix belonged in `clientRoleContainer`,
which `roles-by-id` "already shares" through `clientRoleLocator`. That was
wrong, and a follow-up obeying it would have shipped half a fix: the
`roles-by-id` routes do not go through `clientRoleContainer` at all.
`guardByRoleContainer` in `internal/admin/router.go` resolves the role with
`Roles().ByID` and hands it to `byIDLocator`, so `POST
/roles-by-id/{master-realm client role id}/composites` would have stayed
open. `eachComposite` is the one place the by-name and by-id families meet,
and both are covered by
`TestTheRealmsOwnClientRefusesCompositeWrites`.

**`PUT` and `DELETE` are refused too, on both route families.** Measured
2026-08-27, the four requests F26 asked for, with the full administrator token
and `master-realm`'s `query-groups` as the subject:

```
$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $T' -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"id":"46ed77e7-ff06-4f23-8795-c76a4dffa39b","name":"query-groups","description":"${role_query-groups}","composite":false,"clientRole":true,"containerId":"fdbfea45-9f9b-401a-a114-cf2d0cef3be1","attributes":{}}' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/clients/fdbfea45-9f9b-401a-a114-cf2d0cef3be1/roles/query-groups
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
HTTP 403

$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $T' -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"id":"46ed77e7-ff06-4f23-8795-c76a4dffa39b","name":"query-groups","description":"${role_query-groups}","composite":false,"clientRole":true,"containerId":"fdbfea45-9f9b-401a-a114-cf2d0cef3be1","attributes":{}}' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/roles-by-id/46ed77e7-ff06-4f23-8795-c76a4dffa39b
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
HTTP 403

$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $T' -X DELETE http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/roles-by-id/46ed77e7-ff06-4f23-8795-c76a4dffa39b
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
HTTP 403

$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $T' -X DELETE http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/clients/fdbfea45-9f9b-401a-a114-cf2d0cef3be1/roles/query-groups
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
HTTP 403
```

`query-groups` was read back afterwards and is still there. The `PUT` body is
the role's own representation unchanged, so nothing about the *content* earns
the 403 - the rule is on the container. Both families had to be asked, for the
reason the composite passage above gives: `roles-by-id` does not go through
`clientRoleContainer`.

So "the realm's own client is never configurable" now covers create, both
composite verbs, update and delete, on both the by-name and by-id routes -
every write measured on it so far. **Gloak refuses only create and the two
composite verbs**; F26 in `2026-08-18-gloak-followups.md` is now an
implementation gap rather than an unmeasured one.

#### The realm roles `admin` and `create-realm` refuse `PUT` to a full administrator too

Measured 2026-08-27. These are realm roles, not the realm's own client's, and
they are the same two the caller-relative rule below singles out as realm-level
admin roles:

```
$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $T' -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"id":"4c465654-eddf-422b-ad65-3f85e4679995","name":"admin","description":"${role_admin}","composite":true,"clientRole":false,"containerId":"67280e0f-c53f-413d-9a7c-2e3b245a7b92","attributes":{}}' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/roles/admin
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
HTTP 403

$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $T' -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"id":"86cbdeb9-0b2f-44ae-bbdd-da3f9f93f79f","name":"create-realm","description":"${role_create-realm}","composite":false,"clientRole":false,"containerId":"67280e0f-c53f-413d-9a7c-2e3b245a7b92","attributes":{}}' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/roles/create-realm
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
HTTP 403

$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $T' -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"id":"3b051ec6-9406-4c5b-bcfa-28a44d84147f","name":"offline_access","description":"${role_offline-access}","composite":false,"clientRole":false,"containerId":"67280e0f-c53f-413d-9a7c-2e3b245a7b92","attributes":{}}' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/roles/offline_access
HTTP 204

$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $T' -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"id":"3b051ec6-9406-4c5b-bcfa-28a44d84147f","name":"offline_access","description":"${role_offline-access}","composite":false,"clientRole":false,"containerId":"67280e0f-c53f-413d-9a7c-2e3b245a7b92","attributes":{"probe":["v1"]}}' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/roles/offline_access
HTTP 204
```

The body is the role's own representation unchanged in the first three, so the
403 is not about what is being written. `offline_access`, an ordinary
bootstrapped realm role, takes the same unchanged `PUT` **and** one that adds
an `attributes` key, both 204.

**What that establishes, and what it does not.** It rules out two explanations:
the refusal is not about attributes, and it is not about being bootstrapped,
because `offline_access` is bootstrapped and is not refused. It does **not**
establish that `admin` and `create-realm` are the only two realm roles refused.
`master` has five realm roles; two of them, `default-roles-master` and
`uma_authorization`, were not probed, and the container has been removed, so
this is one negative control and not a sweep. The
narrowest true statement is: these two are refused, `offline_access` is not,
and the boundary between them is unmeasured. That `admin` and `create-realm`
are exactly the two realm roles F28's predicate treats as admin roles is a
suggestive coincidence, not evidence - the pagination rule in `AGENTS.md` is
this document's standing reminder of what generalising from a partial probe
costs. A sweep of all five realm roles would settle it.

This corrects the one line the role-mapping cut's `briefRepresentation` sweep
left behind. That sweep wanted a realm role carrying real attributes, tried
`PUT /roles/admin` and `PUT /roles/create-realm` with an `attributes` key,
got 403 from both, and read it as "attributes cannot be written to a built-in
role" - which is why it went on to create `probe-attr`. Creating `probe-attr`
was the right move for a different reason: the attributes were never what was
refused.

**Gloak does not reproduce either refusal.** `updateRealmRole` has no such
check, so this is an open divergence of the same shape as F26 and is filed
with it.

### Adding an admin role as a composite is judged against the *caller*, not the role

Measured 2026-08-25 against a plain `docker run` of 26.7.1, chasing whether
the realm side has the same escalation the `master-realm` client side had. It
does not, and the reason is a rule this document had not recorded at all.

The suspected path was: a caller holding only `manage-realm` does `POST
/admin/realms/master/roles/default-roles-master/composites` naming the
`admin` role's id. Realm parent, realm child, so the route guard and the
per-child `requiresChildManageRole` check both pass, and `admin` lands on the
role every user in the realm holds. **Keycloak refuses it**, but not because
of anything about `default-roles-master`:

| Caller | Parent | Child | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| full administrator | `default-roles-master` | `admin` | 204 |
| full administrator | `default-roles-master` | `probe-child` (ordinary) | 204 |
| full administrator | `probe-parent` (ordinary) | `admin` | 204 |
| only `manage-realm` | `default-roles-master` | `admin` | **403** |
| only `manage-realm` | `default-roles-master` | `probe-child` (ordinary) | 204 |

So the parent does not matter and the child alone does not matter: **the same
request is 204 for one caller and 403 for another.** The full administrator,
which holds `admin`, may do it; a `manage-realm` holder may not.

Swept over every child a caller holding `manage-realm` **and**
`manage-clients` could name - both roles held so that the cross-family
per-child rule above is not what is being measured - against an ordinary realm
role as the parent, with the full administrator run beside it as a control:

| Child (master-realm client roles) | `manage-realm`+`manage-clients` | full admin |
|---|---|---|
| `create-client` | 204 | 204 |
| `impersonation` | **403** | 204 |
| `manage-authorization` | 204 | 204 |
| `manage-clients` | 204 | 204 |
| `manage-events` | **403** | 204 |
| `manage-identity-providers` | **403** | 204 |
| `manage-organizations` | 204 | 204 |
| `manage-realm` | 204 | 204 |
| `manage-users` | **403** | 204 |
| `query-clients` | 204 | 204 |
| `query-groups` | 204 | 204 |
| `query-organizations` | 204 | 204 |
| `query-realms` | 204 | 204 |
| `query-users` | 204 | 204 |
| `view-authorization` | 204 | 204 |
| `view-clients` | 204 | 204 |
| `view-events` | **403** | 204 |
| `view-identity-providers` | **403** | 204 |
| `view-organizations` | 204 | 204 |
| `view-realm` | 204 | 204 |
| `view-users` | **403** | 204 |

| Child (realm roles) | `manage-realm`+`manage-clients` | full admin |
|---|---|---|
| `admin` | **403** | 204 |
| `create-realm` | **403** | 204 |
| `default-roles-master` | 204 | 204 |
| `offline_access` | 204 | 204 |
| `probe-child` | 204 | 204 |
| `uma_authorization` | 204 | 204 |

Two things this rules out. It is **not** "the caller must hold the child":
the caller held neither `view-realm` nor `probe-child` and got 204 for both.
It is **not** a fixed list of forbidden children either: the full
administrator's column is 204 throughout, and the refused set moves when the
caller changes. Granting the same caller `manage-users` and re-running the
refused set:

| Child | before | after granting `manage-users` |
|---|---|---|
| `view-users` | 403 | **204** |
| `manage-users` | 403 | **204** |
| `query-users` | 204 | 204 |
| `impersonation` | 403 | 403 |
| `view-events` | 403 | 403 |
| `manage-events` | 403 | 403 |
| `view-identity-providers` | 403 | 403 |
| `manage-identity-providers` | 403 | 403 |

The rule is therefore: **a caller may attach an admin role as a composite only
if it already has the administrative power that role confers.** `manage-realm`
carries `view-realm` and the realm-shaped roles with it, `manage-clients`
carries `view-clients`/`create-client`/the authorization pair, `manage-users`
carries `view-users`, every `query-*` role is open to any of these, and
`impersonation`, the events pair, the identity-provider pair, `admin` and
`create-realm` each need themselves. Ordinary, non-admin roles are never
checked this way.

**Gloak does not implement this and answers 204 throughout.** It is a live
privilege-escalation path the moment a narrow-role admin can be minted, and it
is not implemented here on purpose: the naive version - refuse when the caller
does not hold the child - is falsified by fourteen 204s in the table above,
so a partial implementation would diverge in the *too restrictive* direction,
which this branch has already been burned by once on the `DELETE .../composites`
cross-family rule. The same check governs role assignment to a user, which is
a later cut, so it wants one deliberate task covering both. Tracked as F28 in
`2026-08-18-gloak-followups.md`.

**Implemented 2026-08-27**, once the rule below was derived. The paragraph
above describes the state at the time it was written; the section that follows
is the measurement that closed it.

### A caller may hand out a role only if its own rights already confer it

> **Two `$ curl` lines in this section are abbreviated with `...`** - the
> "two `DELETE`s disagree" block writes `curl ... -X DELETE ...` rather than
> the full argv. The ellipses stand in for the `-H 'Authorization: ...'` and
> the host, and the full argv is in the task report. Every other command line
> in this section is rendered from the argv that was executed, `-H` included.

Measured 2026-08-27 against the reference 26.7.1 container on port 18091, to
derive the rule the section above stopped short of. **2005 authorized requests,
each preceded by its own fresh token mint**, each printed from the same argv
list that was then executed rather than re-typed beside it.

The rule, in one sentence:

> **A caller may hand out a role only if the role is not one of the realm's own
> admin roles, or the caller's own effective roles already confer that admin
> role - itself, or one measured to subsume it.**

"Hand out" is four operations and they take **one** predicate:
`GET .../role-mappings/realm/available`,
`GET .../role-mappings/clients/{uuid}/available`, the role-mapping writes on
both locators, and `POST .../composites`. What differs between them is only the
route guard in front, which was already measured.

#### Method: four surfaces, because no one surface admits every caller

The obvious sweep - 22 admin roles as the caller's single role, 27 children
each - cannot be run on one endpoint. Each of these routes has a guard, and
twenty of the 22 roles fail every one of them, so the answer would be the
guard's 403 and not the rule's. Four caller sets were built instead, each
holding the minimum that opens one surface plus the role under test:

| Set | Caller holds | Surface | Baseline it contributes |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | `view-users` + row | both `available` reads | none - `view-users` confers nothing |
| B | `manage-users` + row | role-mapping writes, and the same two reads | what `manage-users` confers |
| C | `manage-realm` + `manage-clients` + row | `POST /roles/m7-parent/composites` | what those two confer |
| D | `manage-clients` + row | `POST /clients/m7-client/roles/m7-cparent/composites` | what `manage-clients` confers |

Three different baselines is what makes the per-role contribution separable: a
conferral hidden inside one set's baseline shows up in another's. Set C
reproduces the two-caller sweep in the section above exactly - same nine
refusals - which is the check that the new fixtures measure the same thing the
old ones did.

#### What each admin role confers

Derived from the matrices below and then checked back against every cell of
them - **3178 cell verdicts, 0 disagreements**. Beyond itself:

| Role | Also confers |
|---|---|
| `manage-realm` | `view-realm`, `manage-organizations` |
| `manage-clients` | `view-clients`, `create-client`, `manage-authorization`, and all five `query-*` |
| `manage-users` | `view-users`, `query-clients`, `query-organizations`, `query-realms` |
| `manage-organizations` | `view-organizations` |
| `manage-authorization` | `view-authorization` |
| `manage-events` | `view-events` |
| `manage-identity-providers` | `view-identity-providers` |
| `view-clients` | `view-authorization`, `query-clients` |
| `view-users` | `query-users`, `query-groups` |
| `view-organizations` | `query-organizations` |
| every other admin role | nothing - `impersonation`, `create-realm`, `admin`, the `query-*` roles, `create-client`, `view-events`, `view-identity-providers`, `view-authorization`, `view-realm` each need themselves |

Two things are deliberately not in that table. **Every role confers itself** -
that is reflexivity, not an entry. And **`admin` has no row** although it
confers everything: it is composite over all 21 client roles plus
`create-realm`, so a holder's effective set already carries them and the
closure reaches the rest.

**One family of edges is unobservable and is recorded only where it was
measured directly.** Whether a role confers the `query-*` roles cannot be asked
of most roles: naming a client role as a composite child needs `manage-clients`
(the parent-side guard, or the cross-family per-child rule), and a role-mapping
write needs `manage-users`. Both of those confer all five, so no reachable
request can distinguish whether, say, `manage-events` confers `query-realms`.
The two rows above are the two that were measured on their own.

#### `create-realm` needs itself, measured on the two surfaces whose matrix has no row for it

Matrix D below carries an `m7d-create-realm` row; matrices B and C do not,
because `create-realm` alone opens neither of those surfaces. So the caller
sets that do open them were given `create-realm` as their extra role and probed
directly. Both refuse `admin`, both allow `create-realm` itself, and both allow
an ordinary realm role - which is the whole predicate on one row:

```
--- P1 m7b-create-realm assign admin (caller m7b-create-realm) ---
$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $CT' -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '[{"id": "4c465654-eddf-422b-ad65-3f85e4679995", "name": "admin"}]' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/users/d2d0ed3f-ff8b-47f7-bd1d-d53e5ad1f534/role-mappings/realm
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
HTTP 403

--- P1 m7b-create-realm assign create-realm (caller m7b-create-realm) ---
$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $CT' -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '[{"id": "86cbdeb9-0b2f-44ae-bbdd-da3f9f93f79f", "name": "create-realm"}]' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/users/d2d0ed3f-ff8b-47f7-bd1d-d53e5ad1f534/role-mappings/realm

HTTP 204

--- P1 m7b-create-realm assign m7-child (caller m7b-create-realm) ---
$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $CT' -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '[{"id": "2fa6c637-d0c7-42f6-86a9-3107d4913201", "name": "m7-child"}]' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/users/d2d0ed3f-ff8b-47f7-bd1d-d53e5ad1f534/role-mappings/realm

HTTP 204

--- P1 m7c-create-realm composite admin (caller m7c-create-realm) ---
$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $CT' -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '[{"id": "4c465654-eddf-422b-ad65-3f85e4679995", "name": "admin"}]' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/roles/m7-parent/composites
{"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
HTTP 403

--- P1 m7c-create-realm composite create-realm (caller m7c-create-realm) ---
$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $CT' -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '[{"id": "86cbdeb9-0b2f-44ae-bbdd-da3f9f93f79f", "name": "create-realm"}]' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/roles/m7-parent/composites

HTTP 204

--- P1 m7c-create-realm composite m7-child (caller m7c-create-realm) ---
$ curl -s -o /dev/stdout -w '\nHTTP %{http_code}\n' -H 'Authorization: Bearer $CT' -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '[{"id": "2fa6c637-d0c7-42f6-86a9-3107d4913201", "name": "m7-child"}]' http://localhost:18091/admin/realms/master/roles/m7-parent/composites

HTTP 204
```

`m7b-create-realm` is `manage-users` + `create-realm`, `m7c-create-realm` is
`manage-realm` + `manage-clients` + `create-realm`, and `m7-child` is an
ordinary realm role of the sweep's own making.
