```
  {"realm":"p4e","displayName":"Realm E","displayNameHtml":"<b>E</b>","registrationEmailAsUsername":false,"bruteForceProtected":false,"supportedLocales":[],"organizationsEnabled":false}

create-client | manage-authorization | manage-clients | manage-events |
manage-identity-providers | manage-organizations | query-clients | query-groups |
query-organizations | query-realms | query-users | view-authorization |
view-clients | view-events | view-identity-providers | view-organizations
  {"realm":"p4e","displayName":"Realm E","displayNameHtml":"<b>E</b>","bruteForceProtected":false,"supportedLocales":[],"organizationsEnabled":false}

impersonation | no admin role
  403 {"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
```

**`supportedLocales` is in the two short shapes and not in the long one.** The
104-key body has no such key when `internationalizationEnabled` is false; the
short bodies emit `[]`. One field with `omitempty` produces neither.

**`impersonation` is the one admin role of the 21 that answers 403 here.**

### The listing returns full representations, and shrinks per caller

`GET /admin/realms` defaults to `briefRepresentation=false` - the opposite of
the role listings - and returns an array of the 104-key body.
`transfer-encoding: chunked`, `Content-Type: application/json;charset=UTF-8`,
`Cache-Control: no-cache`.

```
?briefRepresentation=true
[{"id":"...","realm":"p4b","enabled":true},
 {"id":"...","realm":"master","displayName":"Keycloak","displayNameHtml":"<div class=\"kc-logo-text\"><span>Keycloak</span></div>","enabled":true}]
```

A caller without `view-realm` gets a **one-key** entry and
`briefRepresentation` does nothing to it - absent and `true` gave byte-identical
bodies:

```
[{"realm":"p4e"}]
```

One parameter, three answers. That is the third such parameter on this API.

The listing is **not sorted**. Ten realms came back `probe-new-3, p4id, p4off,
p4c, p4e, p4put, master, p4rich, p4a, p4d` - neither alphabetical nor creation
order - and identically on a second call.

`impersonation` and a caller with no admin role are 403 on the listing. So is a
caller holding only the realm role `create-realm`, which gets **200** on the
single read - the collection's two verbs disagree about who may use them in both
directions, since `create-realm` is also the only role `POST` accepts.

### What a created realm is bootstrapped with

```
clients      account, account-console, admin-cli, broker, realm-management, security-admin-console
realm roles  default-roles-p4a (composite), offline_access, uma_authorization
users        none
groups       none
keys         RSA/RS256 SIG, RSA/RSA-OAEP ENC, OCT/HS512 SIG, OCT/AES ENC
```

`admin` and `create-realm` exist in `master` alone. `realm-management` owns
**22** roles where `master-realm` owns 21; the extra one is `realm-admin`,
composite over the other 21. `view-clients`, `view-users` and
`view-organizations` carry the same composites they do in master.

`default-roles-p4a` is composite over `offline_access`, `uma_authorization` and
`account`'s `manage-account` and `view-profile` - the same four master's is.

### Creating a realm creates a client in master, and edits master's `admin` role

Creating `p4a` creates `p4a-realm` in **master**: bearer-only, named `p4a
Realm`, owning **21** roles and no `realm-admin`, with `defaultClientScopes` and
`optionalClientScopes` both `[]` where `master-realm`'s carry the usual six and
five, and with no `protocol` key.

And master's realm role `admin` gains all 21 of them as composites: 22 on a
fresh install, 43 after one realm, 127 after five. Deleting the realm removes
the client and takes the 21 back out - measured 127 down to 106 on the same
container.

### Rights are resolved against one container, chosen by the token's realm

The question P4 turns on, measured four ways.

| caller | token realm | route | result |
|---|---|---|---|
| `view-users` on `master-realm` | master | `GET /admin/realms/master` | 200, five-key |
| `view-users` on `master-realm` | master | `GET /admin/realms/p4e` | 200, four-key |
| `view-users` on `master-realm` | master | `GET /admin/realms/master/users` | 200 |
| `view-users` on `master-realm` | master | `GET /admin/realms/p4e/users` | **403** |
| `view-users` on `master-realm` | master | `GET /admin/realms/p4e/clients` | **403** |
| `view-users` on `master-realm` | master | `GET /admin/realms/p4e/roles` | **403** |
| `view-users` on `master-realm` | master | `GET /admin/realms` | `[{"realm":"master"}]` |
| `view-users` on `p4e-realm` | master | `GET /admin/realms/p4e/users` | 200 |
| `view-users` on `p4e-realm` | master | `GET /admin/realms/master/users` | **403** |
| `view-users` on `p4e-realm` | master | `GET /admin/realms` | `[{"realm":"p4e"}]` |
| `realm-admin` on `realm-management` | p4e | `GET /admin/realms/p4e` | 200, full |
| `realm-admin` on `realm-management` | p4e | `GET /admin/realms/master` | **403** |
| `realm-admin` on `realm-management` | p4e | `GET /admin/realms/p4a` | **403** |
| `realm-admin` on `realm-management` | p4e | `POST /admin/realms` | **403** |

So: **every route resolves the caller's rights against exactly one container -
`{realm}-realm` in master for a master-issued token, `realm-management` in the
realm itself for a realm-issued one - and `GET /admin/realms/{realm}` is the one
place a master caller's rights leak sideways, and only in its shortest form.**
Nothing reaches upwards: the strongest role a non-master realm has is 403 on
master.

The token carries none of this. `iss` is the issuing realm's, `aud` is absent
and `resource_access` is empty, because `admin-cli` is a lightweight client. The
roles are resolved from storage at request time.

### Which role each realm operation needs

One caller per role on a realm `p4d`, a fresh token minted immediately before
every call.

```
role                       GET      listing entry   PUT   DELETE
view-realm                 200 full full            403   -
manage-realm               200 full full            204   204
realm-admin                200 full full            204   204
view-users                 200 5key {"realm":...}   403   -
manage-users               200 5key {"realm":...}   403   -
create-client              200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
manage-authorization       200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
manage-clients             200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
manage-events              200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
manage-identity-providers  200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
manage-organizations       200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
query-clients              200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
query-groups               200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
query-organizations        200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
query-realms               200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
query-users                200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
view-authorization         200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
view-clients               200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
view-events                200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
view-identity-providers    200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
view-organizations         200 4key {"realm":...}   403   -
impersonation              403      403             403   -
no admin role              403      403             403   -
```

`DELETE` was measured separately, on `manage-realm` held in master through
`p4b-realm` (204) and on `manage-realm` held inside the realm through
`realm-management` (204). `POST /admin/realms` takes the realm role
`create-realm` in master and refuses `manage-realm`, `realm-admin` and every
client role.

### The realm is resolved before the caller is judged

```
GET|PUT|DELETE /admin/realms/nosuchrealm  ->  404 {"error":"Realm not found."}
```

Every caller, every verb, including one holding no admin role at all - measured
on seven callers in master and inside another realm. A realm that **does** exist
and the caller may not see answers 403. So the resource comes first, the same
order `/groups/{id}` and `/roles-by-id/{id}` take, and the opposite of the users
family's coarse gate.

### `PUT` merges, renames, and replaces exactly one field

A body of `{}` answers 204 and changes nothing. `{"realm":"p4z"}` on
`/admin/realms/p4a` answers 204 and **renames the realm**, keeping its id;
`GET /admin/realms/p4a` is then 404. `{"displayName":null}` is ignored where
`{"displayName":""}` writes an empty string that the body then carries.

`attributes` is replaced rather than merged, and not cleanly. A realm created
with `{"a":"1","b":"2"}` carried those two plus the eight defaults. After
`PUT {"attributes":{"c":"3"}}` the map held `c` and seven derived policy
attributes: `a`, `b` **and `realmReusableOtpCode`** were all gone. So the result
is neither what was sent nor what was there.

`POST` honours an `id` in the body and creates the realm with it. `POST`'s
`attributes` **merge** with the defaults where `PUT`'s replace.

### A rename moves two things and deliberately leaves a third

Measured on `rn1` renamed to `rn2`:

```
master's clients   rn1-realm  ->  rn2-realm
master's admin     43 composites  ->  43 composites   (untouched)
rn2's account      baseUrl /realms/rn2/account/, redirectUris ["/realms/rn2/account/*"]
rn2's realm roles  ["default-roles-rn1", "uma_authorization", "offline_access"]
rn2's defaultRole  default-roles-rn1
```

So the `{realm}-realm` client in master follows the name, the three clients
whose URLs carry the realm name are rewritten, and **`default-roles-{old}` keeps
its old name** - as does the `defaultRole` block that points at it. That last
one is the part of a rename that looks like an oversight and is the contract; a
build that renamed the role too would diverge on every subsequent read of the
realm.

### The error shapes, and there are three families

```
POST   /admin/realms, duplicate          -> 409 {"errorMessage":"Realm p4a already exists"}
POST   /admin/realms, {"realm":"master"} -> 409 {"errorMessage":"Realm master already exists"}
POST   /admin/realms, {}                 -> 400 {"errorMessage":"Realm name cannot be empty"}
POST   /admin/realms, no body            -> 400 {"errorMessage":"unable to read contents from stream"}
POST   /admin/realms, not JSON           -> 400 {"errorMessage":"unable to read contents from stream"}
POST   /admin/realms, no Content-Type    -> 415 {"error":"The content-type header value did not match the value in @Consumes"}
PUT    /admin/realms/{r}, not JSON       -> 400 {"error":"invalid_request","error_description":"Cannot parse the JSON"}
PUT    /admin/realms/{r}, no body        -> 500 {"error":"unknown_error","error_description":"For more on this error consult the server log."}
PUT    /admin/realms/{r}, unknown field  -> 400 {"error":"Invalid json representation for RealmRepresentation. Unrecognized field \"nosuchfield\" at line 1 column 20."}
PUT    /admin/realms/{r}, name taken     -> 409 {"errorMessage":"Realm with same name exists"}
GET|PUT|DELETE /admin/realms/{gone}      -> 404 {"error":"Realm not found."}
DELETE /admin/realms/master              -> 400 {"errorMessage":"Can't remove master realm"}
no or bad token                          -> 401 {"error":"HTTP 401 Unauthorized"}
wrong role                               -> 403 {"error":"HTTP 403 Forbidden"}
```

`POST` and `PUT` answer the same malformed body in two different families, and
an absent body as a 400 and a **500** respectively. The unknown-field 400
carries a Jackson message with a line and column in it, so its text moves when
the client reformats its JSON.

Renaming onto a taken name is `Realm with same name exists`; creating one is
`Realm p4a already exists`. Two collisions of one resource, two wordings.

### A wrong method here refutes "the verb decides"

```
POST   /admin/realms/{realm}  -> 404 {"error":"HTTP 404 Not Found"}
PATCH  /admin/realms/{realm}  -> 405 {"error":"HTTP 405 Method Not Allowed"}
PUT    /admin/realms          -> 405 {"error":"HTTP 405 Method Not Allowed"}
DELETE /admin/realms          -> 405 {"error":"HTTP 405 Method Not Allowed"}
HEAD   /admin/realms/{realm}  -> 200
```

"A wrong method is not always 404" above concluded from the role-mapping paths
that the status is decided by the verb: `POST` and `DELETE` 404, `PUT` and
`PATCH` 405. **A `DELETE` answering 405 refutes it.** Neither the path nor the
verb explains all four. See F31; nothing is changed in Gloak on the strength of
this, only the second counter-example recorded.

### A disabled realm still answers on the protocol side

```
$ curl -s -o body -D hdr -w 'status=%{http_code}\n' http://localhost:8084/realms/p4off
status=200
Content-Type: application/json;charset=UTF-8
{"realm":"p4off","public_key":"MIIBIjANBgkqhkiG9w0BAQEFAAOCAQ8A...
```

`"enabled":false` does not take the realm off the network.

### A user in a created realm cannot log in without a full profile

```
$ curl -s -X POST http://localhost:8084/realms/p4c/protocol/openid-connect/token \
    -d grant_type=password -d client_id=admin-cli -d username=c-admin -d password=pw
{"error":"invalid_grant","error_description":"Account is not fully set up"}
```

The same user with `email`, `firstName`, `lastName` and `emailVerified` set
obtains a token. `VERIFY_PROFILE` is enabled and not a default action in both
`master` and a created realm - the two realms' `required-actions` listings are
identical - so what differs is the user, not the realm: `master`'s bootstrapped
administrator carries `is_temporary_admin`. Every P4 probe therefore creates
callers with a complete profile. Gloak reproduces none of this and is measurably
more permissive on that one path; it is P8's.

## Realm keys

`GET /admin/realms/{realm}/keys`, measured 2026-08-29. 200,
`Content-Type: application/json;charset=UTF-8`, `Cache-Control: no-cache`, the
five security headers. Identical on `master` and on a realm created through
`POST /admin/realms`.

```
{"active":{"RSA-OAEP":"<kid>","HS512":"<kid>","RS256":"<kid>","AES":"<kid>"},
 "keys":[ ...four... ]}
```

One key entry carries these keys in this declared order:

```
providerId, providerPriority, kid, status, type, algorithm,
publicKey, certificate, use, validTo
```

| type | algorithm | use | publicKey / certificate / validTo | kid |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| RSA | RS256 | SIG | present | 43-char base64url |
| RSA | RSA-OAEP | ENC | present | 43-char base64url |
| OCT | HS512 | SIG | **absent** | UUID |
| OCT | AES | ENC | **absent** | UUID |

`providerPriority` is 100 and `status` is `ACTIVE` on all four. `validTo` is the
certificate's `notAfter` in milliseconds. **`use` is upper case here and lower
case in the JWKS** for the same two keys.

**The RSA `kid` is `base64url(sha256(SubjectPublicKeyInfo))`, unpadded.**
Master's recorded `publicKey`

```
MIIBIjANBgkqhkiG9w0BAQEFAAOCAQ8AMIIBCgKCAQEAwZf5VMa5NxHtHj7d7n5p6bMbsiQwg6FEW75o9QhDIGPJAXP3TUOuKyy65Ww+wHLWiF8rzj2XbZO9coUBu5wD8KTjm2/KKgne2pVcEk4XRFEcLrlrZmqmIybXDfUREbWrpVBRB1F1R6R3G89swrw7Gm10CS4qOsg/RLAD0QAVp/86LatxutvHsCGS62EK9uBoruylAdMUKk7DvLyMw2TPCd6Lc8EXkz13zNgzf+8aL/m7t7eYA4nKAgMPoG86jT7b23KvECYw0Q1yYGBcCiarHDLEkFogIejZGw7KT6+FHS7fCKsKbAPZ+wIvLcoYtEvvgasV3DRXtvuYynWm00665wIDAQAB
```

digests to its recorded `kid` `Q80zap21IG6Jjn3zecYt3iXqDNthWiPlL4dNVvQGkyw`.
**It is not the RFC 7638 JWK thumbprint**, which was computed first over the
same key and gives `RI0Cq8BR5aI1Km8s8ioVX63uTGMEWZOCfyf1NN6jy7I`. The pair is a
vector in `internal/keys/keys_test.go`.

**`active` is a Java map and `internal/javamap` reproduces it.** Measured
`RSA-OAEP, HS512, RS256, AES` on both realms, which is what `javamap.KeyOrder`
returns. This is its **sixth** confirmed key set and the **first with no bucket
collision at all**.

**The `keys` array is ordered by `providerId`, which is a random UUID.** master
came back `501bb07d, c64d81a4, c801ee0a, cdaa5860` and a created realm
`3a8bb3b7, 5ab0fbec, bf17f8cb, df836b05`, both ascending and with different
algorithm orders. `providerId` is the id of the key *provider component*, a
different value from the `kid` on every measured key.

Guard: `view-realm` or `manage-realm`, swept across all 22 `realm-management`
roles and a caller holding none. An unknown realm is 404
`{"error":"Realm not found."}`; no token is 401.

## Default groups

```
GET    /admin/realms/{r}/default-groups            -> 200, application/json;charset=UTF-8, no-cache
PUT    /admin/realms/{r}/default-groups/{groupId}  -> 204, Cache-Control: no-cache, no X-Frame-Options
DELETE /admin/realms/{r}/default-groups/{groupId}  -> 204, same headers
PUT|DELETE with an unknown group id                -> 404 {"error":"Group not found"}
```

The listing entry is `{"id","name","path",["parentId"],"subGroups":[]}` - the
same shape a user's group listing sends. **No `subGroupCount`, no `attributes`,
no `access`**, and `briefRepresentation=false` gives a byte-identical body.

`PUT` is idempotent: twice gives 204 twice and one entry. `DELETE` of a group
that is not a default group is 204, not 404. Deleting the group itself removes
it from the listing.

**The listing has no reproducible order.** Three groups added `zzz, aaa, mmm`
came back in that order; in another realm a parent added first and a child
second came back child first. Neither insertion order, name, id nor path
explains both.

**The `PUT` and `DELETE` answer 403, not 404, for a group that does not
exist** - to a `view-realm` caller, which may read this listing but not write
it, and to a caller holding nothing. That inverts the rule every `Groups`-tagged
route follows, and it follows the description's **tag** rather than the presence
of a group in the path.

Guard: `view-realm` or `manage-realm` to read, `manage-realm` to write.

**A created realm's new users join its default groups.** `POST /users` in a
realm holding two of them answered 201, and `GET /users/{id}/groups` came back
with both.

## `GET /admin/realms/{realm}/group-by-path/{path}`

200, `application/json;charset=UTF-8`, `Cache-Control: no-cache`. The body is
the single group read **minus its `access` block** and identical otherwise,
measured side by side on the same group:

```
GET /groups/{id}      {"id","name","path","subGroupCount":1,"subGroups":[],"attributes":{},"realmRoles":[],"clientRoles":{},"access":{...}}
GET /group-by-path/g1 {"id","name","path","subGroupCount":1,"subGroups":[],"attributes":{},"realmRoles":[],"clientRoles":{}}
```

That is a **sixth** representation of one group. `briefRepresentation` does
nothing to it.

A leading slash is optional: `/group-by-path/g1` and `/group-by-path/%2Fg1` both
answer 200 for the same group. A nested path walks down. A path resolving to
nothing, the empty one included, is

```
404 {"error":"Group path does not exist"}   Content-Type: application/json, no Cache-Control
```

a **twelfth** spelling of not-found on the admin API and the **third** for a
group.

Guard: `view-users` or `manage-users`. **`query-groups` does not open it**,
although it opens the group listing, and `manage-realm` - which opens the
default groups next door - is 403.

## Client policies and client profiles

```
GET  /admin/realms/{r}/client-policies/policies                              -> {"policies":[]}
GET  /admin/realms/{r}/client-policies/policies?include-global-policies=true -> {"policies":[],"globalPolicies":[]}
GET  /admin/realms/{r}/client-policies/profiles                              -> {"profiles":[]}
GET  /admin/realms/{r}/client-policies/profiles?include-global-profiles=true -> {"profiles":[],"globalProfiles":[ ...ten... ]}
PUT  either                                                                  -> 204, X-Frame-Options, and NO Cache-Control
```

The ten global profiles are `fapi-1-baseline`, `fapi-1-advanced`, `fapi-ciba`,
`fapi-2-security-profile`, `fapi-2-message-signing`,
`oauth-2-1-for-confidential-client`, `oauth-2-1-for-public-client`,
`fapi-2-dpop-security-profile`, `fapi-2-dpop-message-signing`,
`saml-security-profile`, 9286 bytes in total. They are recorded verbatim at
`internal/admin/globalclientprofiles.json`; several of their `configuration`
objects have keys that are **not** in alphabetical order, so they are served as
bytes rather than marshalled from Go. 26.7.1 ships **no** global policies.

Shapes:

```
profile  {"name","description"?,"executors":[{"executor","configuration"}]}
policy   {"name","description"?,"enabled"?,"conditions":[{"condition","configuration"}],"profiles":[...]}
```

A profile written without a description comes back without the key; a policy
written without `enabled` comes back without it.

Errors - **three families on one endpoint**:

```
malformed body    -> 400 {"error":"invalid_request","error_description":"Cannot parse the JSON"}
no body at all    -> 400 {"errorMessage":"Passing null clientProfiles not allowed"}
unknown field     -> 400 {"error":"Invalid json representation for ClientProfilesRepresentation. Unrecognized field \"nosuchfield\" at line 1 column 20."}
unknown executor  -> 400 {"errorMessage":"proposed client profile contains the executor, which does not have valid provider, or has invalid configuration."}
policy naming an
unknown profile   -> 400 {"errorMessage":"Policy pol-b contains invalid profile nope"}
```

The no-body case is a 400 here and a **500** on `PUT /admin/realms/{realm}` -
same verb, neighbouring routes on one resource, two answers.

Guard: `view-realm` or `manage-realm` to read, `manage-realm` to write.

**They are the realm representation's own `clientProfiles` and
`clientPolicies`**, measured in both directions: a `PUT` here changes what
`GET /admin/realms/{realm}` answers, and `PUT /admin/realms/{realm}` carrying
`clientProfiles` changes what this route answers. `PUT /admin/realms/{r}` with
`{"clientProfiles":{}}` **clears** them to `[]`, and a `PUT` carrying a profile
**replaces** rather than merging: a profile sent without a description leaves
the stored description gone.

## Client types

```
GET|PUT /admin/realms/{realm}/client-types
501 {"error":"Feature not enabled","error_description":"For more on this error consult the server log."}
Content-Type: application/json, no Cache-Control
```

`CLIENT_TYPES` is a disabled preview feature on a default 26.7.1, the same
situation as `GET .../client-secret/rotated`'s permanent 404. The ordering was
measured on four callers: **no token is 401, a bad token is 401, an unknown
realm with a valid token is 404 `Realm not found.`, and then every authenticated
caller gets the 501 - including one holding no admin role at all.** So the
feature check sits between the realm and the authorization check, and this route
has no role list.

## Incidental, not chased

A double slash in an admin path answers
`{"error":"missingNormalization","error_description":"Request path not normalized"}`.
Found while a probe built a path from an empty variable. Nothing depends on it
and nothing reproduces it.

## The logout endpoint

Measured 2026-08-29 against a live 26.7.1, sixty-odd probes. **Six response
shapes**, and which one a request gets is not decided by the parameter the
earlier recording blamed.

```
302 to post_logout_redirect_uri   a validated logout with a target
400 theme error page              three distinct rejections
200 theme "Logging out" page      no hint, no target, and a live session
200 theme "You are logged out"    a valid hint and no target
204 empty                         POST carrying a refresh_token
400/401 JSON                      the POST family's rejections
```

### The variable is the browser session, not the `id_token_hint`

The earlier note in this document read "logout without an `id_token_hint` does
not redirect. It serves the theme's `Logging out` confirmation page, 200,
whatever the `post_logout_redirect_uri` is." That measurement was taken through
a cookie jar, and **the jar was the variable**:

```
live session, no hint          200, the "Logging out" confirmation page, and nothing is ended
                               (the refresh token still works afterwards)
no session, no hint,
  client_id + registered target  302
valid hint, no target          200, "You are logged out", and the session IS ended
```

A conformance case had been filed `Pending` with "this response is a theme page"
as its reason on the strength of the first row. It is a redirect.

### The rejection order, and one step is not where it looks

**The `id_token_hint` is checked before the redirect URI.** A garbage hint with
an unregistered URI answers about the hint; a good hint with an unregistered URI
answers about the URI. Every way the hint can fail is one page - rubbish, an
access or refresh token in its place, a bad signature, a rewritten payload, and
another realm's issuer all answer `Invalid parameter: id_token_hint`.

A target sent with no client to validate it against is refused, which covers an
absent `client_id`, an unknown one, and a hint whose client has been deleted.

**A rejected logout ends nothing.** Every rejection above leaves the session
alive, so a failed logout is not a partial one.

### `post.logout.redirect.uris` is a filter over `redirectUris`

Measured across six clients differing only in this attribute:

```
absent          the client's redirectUris
""              the client's redirectUris
"+"             the client's redirectUris
"-"             nothing at all; every target is refused,
                including the client's own redirectUris and the literal "-"
anything else   that ##-separated pattern list, and NOT redirectUris
```

So setting the attribute can only ever **narrow** what a client accepts, never
widen it. And `-` is a marker for the whole value rather than for an entry:
inside a `##` list it is an ordinary relative pattern, accepted and resolved
against the server's base URL (`post_logout_redirect_uri=-` answered
`Location: http://127.0.0.1:8092/-`).

### What this endpoint forgives that `/auth` does not

```
expired id_token_hint          logs out and redirects
hint naming a spent session    the same 302, not an error
disabled client                redirects, where /auth answers the 400 page
a duplicated parameter         not an error at all; the first value wins
```

`/auth` answers `duplicated parameter` for any key sent twice, including one it
never reads. Four probes here - a good and a bad `post_logout_redirect_uri`
together, `state` twice, `id_token_hint` twice and an unread key twice - gave
four successes.

What it does **not** forgive is a `client_id` disagreeing with the hint's `azp`:
that answers `Invalid parameter: id_token_hint`, not a client error.

### Three differences from `/auth` that a reader would assume away

- **The redirect carries `state` and nothing else.** No `iss`, where `/auth`'s
  redirect carries one.
- **An empty `state=` is dropped**, where `/auth` echoes it back as `state=`.
  One parameter, two endpoints, opposite answers to the same input.
- **The page family carries `Cache-Control: no-cache`**, where `/auth`'s page
  family carries none at all. Measured side by side on one container.

### `POST` is two endpoints wearing one path

The `refresh_token` decides which. With one, the request is
client-authenticated, answers **204** with `Cache-Control: no-cache` and a
`Content-Security-Policy`, ignores any `post_logout_redirect_uri` it was given,
and answers 204 again on a replay. A `POST` carrying both a `refresh_token` and
an `id_token_hint` answers 204, so the refresh token decides and not the hint.
Without one it falls through to the `GET` families.

The `GET` family authenticates no client at all, so the same confidential client
that must send its secret on the `POST` redirects without one on the `GET`.

Its two rejection descriptions:

```
Invalid refresh token
Invalid refresh token. Token client and authorized client don't match
```

### The verbs

```
GET      the families above
POST     the families above, body-sourced
PUT      405 {"error":"HTTP 405 Method Not Allowed"}, application/json
DELETE   405, the same
PATCH    405, the same
OPTIONS  200, and NO Allow header
```

`/auth` answers `OPTIONS` with `Allow: HEAD, POST, GET, OPTIONS`. The two
endpoints disagree, which is a fourth data point for F31 and resolves none of
the other three. The 405 **body** is measured here for the first time, and
independently confirmed the same day on the Admin API's client-scope routes by
a branch that never shared a container.

## Client scopes

Measured 2026-08-29 and 2026-08-30. `Client Scopes` is ten operations and
`client-templates` is a **path alias** for all of them: every operation serves
what its sibling serves byte for byte, with one exception -
`POST /client-templates` answers a `Location` under `/client-templates`, so the
header is built from `r.URL.Path` and not from a constant.

### A realm's fifteen scopes are identical in every realm

A realm created through `POST /admin/realms` gets the same fifteen scopes as
master, the same thirty-five protocol mappers, the same attributes and the same
two default sets, byte for byte once the UUIDs are stripped. They are recorded
at `internal/bootstrap/clientscopes.json`.

**The realm's default set is nine, not the six a client carries.** The three
SAML scopes are in the realm's set and are filtered out when an
`openid-connect` client inherits.

### Three shapes, decided by the route rather than by `briefRepresentation`

```
/client-scopes                        6 keys
/default-default-client-scopes        3 keys  (id, name, protocol)
/clients/{uuid}/default-client-scopes 2 keys  (id, name) - protocol omitted
                                              even on scopes that have one
```

A shared serialiser would be wrong on two of the three. `protocolMappers` is
**absent** rather than empty - `offline_access` is the one bootstrapped scope
with no mappers and its representation has five keys where every other has six -
while `attributes` is always present and `{}` when empty. Two neighbouring keys,
opposite rules.

### Inheritance at create is all-or-nothing across the pair

Nine creation bodies:

```
{}                                                  6 defaults, 5 optionals
{"defaultClientScopes":null}                        6 defaults, 5 optionals
{"optionalClientScopes":null}                       6 defaults, 5 optionals
{"defaultClientScopes":["email"]}                   ["email"], and [] optionals
{"defaultClientScopes":[]}                          [], []
{"optionalClientScopes":["phone"]}                  [] defaults, ["phone"]
{"optionalClientScopes":[]}                         [], []
{"default...":["email"],"optional...":["phone"]}    exactly those
{"default...":null,"optional...":["phone"]}         [], ["phone"]
```

**Naming either list - as an array, empty or not - suppresses inheritance on
both.** A per-list nil check gives the realm's five optionals to a client that
asked for one default and no optionals.

Inheritance is filtered by the client's protocol: a `saml` client created bare
inherits `AuthnContextClassRef`, `role_list` and `saml_organization` and no
optionals. `PUT /clients/{uuid}` ignores both lists outright, so they are
write-once at create and only the four dedicated routes move them afterwards.

An unknown scope **name** at create time is dropped in silence: 201 and an empty
list.

### The attachment is one row with a flag, and the two verbs disagree

`PUT .../default-client-scopes/{id}` naming a scope the client already holds as
an **optional** answers 204 and moves nothing. `DELETE .../default-client-scopes/{id}`
**ignores the list its own path names** and removes the scope from whichever
list holds it. Measured on the client routes and on the realm's alike.

A scope of the wrong protocol is a silent no-op: a `saml` scope offered to an
`openid-connect` client answers 204 and attaches nothing.

The realm's `PUT` is a **409** on the repeat where the client's is a 204:

```
PUT /admin/realms/{r}/default-default-client-scopes/{id}  twice
  -> {"error":"conflict","error_description":"Duplicate resource error"}
PUT /clients/{uuid}/default-client-scopes/{id}            twice
  -> 204, 204
```

and putting into one of the realm's lists a scope already in the other is the
same 409, which is what says the two lists are one row.

### Creating a scope: the name is looked at twice

An **absent** `name` is a 500 `unknown_error` - Keycloak's own defect, the same
family as an empty body on `POST /users` - and is checked first. A **present but
empty** name is a 400 naming the empty string and is checked last. Between them
sits the protocol check, which is why `{}` answers about the name and
`{"name":"x"}` answers about the protocol. An absent protocol and an invalid one
both give `Unexpected protocol`, so that check is membership rather than
presence.

The body's `id` wins on create, here and on `POST /clients`: a create naming an
id produced an object with exactly that id and put it in `Location`.

### Guards, and three resolution orders on one resource

The whole family is authorised out of the **clients** role set, including the
two routes the description tags `Realms Admin`: `view-realm` and `manage-realm`
are 403 on `default-default-client-scopes` and `default-optional-client-scopes`
on both verbs, while `view-clients` reads and `manage-clients` writes.
`query-clients` gets the listing as **200 and `[]`** rather than 403.

```
/client-scopes/{id}                  scope, then role   404 for missing, 403 for present
/default-*-client-scopes/{id}        role, then scope   403 for the same missing scope
/clients/{u}/*-client-scopes/{s}     client, role, scope
```

and the missing scope has two spellings on top of that: `Could not find client
scope` from the first family and `Client scope not found` from the other two.

### A whole Admin API route family answers a real 405

`PUT` and `DELETE` on `/admin/realms/{r}/client-scopes`, and `POST` and `PATCH`
on `/admin/realms/{r}/client-scopes/{id}`, all four answer

```
405 {"error":"HTTP 405 Method Not Allowed"}
application/json, the five security headers, NO Allow, no Cache-Control
```

Every previous counter-example to the 404 rule was a mixture within one path
family and every one was on the **protocol** side. This is the first on the
Admin API and the first where a whole route family answers 405 uniformly. It is
the fifth data point and it still does not say what the rule is. See F31.

## The browser login, end to end

Measured 2026-08-30 across sixty-odd probes on one container. This is the flow
between `GET /auth` and `POST /login-actions/authenticate`: which values are
minted where, which are echoed, which are checked, and what each does when it
is wrong.

### One value carries four observables, and it is minted before the password

The authentication session's **root id** is created by `GET /auth`. Measured
three ways at once on a single login:

```
AUTH_SESSION_ID base64-decodes to  "<root id>.<86 chars>"
the redirect's session_state is    <root id>
KEYCLOAK_IDENTITY's sid is         <root id>
the authorization code's 2nd part  <root id>
```

So `session_state` is decided **before any credential is seen**. A design that
mints it when the password verifies gets four observables wrong at once, and no
conformance case would catch it: every case in this flow masks `Location` and
`Set-Cookie`.

The code's three parts are `<uuid-shaped>.<root id>.<client's internal UUID>`,
and its lifespan is 60 seconds.

### The login form's five parameters

`session_code`, `execution`, `client_id`, `tab_id`, `client_data`.

**`client_data` is parsed and then ignored.** It carries the redirect URI, the
response type, the response mode and the state, and none of them is used:

```
client_data naming another redirect_uri  -> redirects to the registered one
client_data naming another state         -> echoes the original
client_data adding rm=fragment           -> parameters still in the query
client_data dropped entirely             -> succeeds
client_data=!!!!                         -> 400 Invalid Request
```

The last row is what says it is parsed. It is a restart hint the browser
carries, never an authority.

### Parameters from the query, credentials from the body

```
all five parameters in the body, empty query   -> the client error page
the same five on POST /auth's query            -> the error page (the body works)
```

Mirror-image rules on two endpoints in one flow. `r.Form` merges query and body
and hides both.

**A repeated parameter is not an error here.** `zz` twice, `tab_id` twice, and
`session_code` twice with the second value garbage all log in - the first value
wins. `/auth` is the only one of the three endpoints in this family that
answers `duplicated parameter`.

### `GET /login-actions/authenticate` is not a read

It attempts the login with whatever credentials the body carries - none, for a
GET - and answers 200 with the page re-served, `Invalid username or password.`,
and the `session_code` **rotated**.

### A failed credential rotates the session code and nothing else

`execution`, `tab_id` and `client_data` are unchanged, the username is echoed
back into the input, the retry with the rotated code succeeds, and the old code
takes the restart branch.

**The disabled-account message is checked after the password.** A disabled user
with the right password gets `Account is disabled, contact your
administrator.`; the same user with a wrong password gets `Invalid username or
password.` like everybody else. Checking `enabled` first turns the form into an
account-enumeration oracle.

### Two tabs share one authentication session

A second `GET /auth` on one browser sets **one** cookie, not three: the session
is reused and only `KC_RESTART` moves. Both tabs have their own `tab_id` and
`session_code`, and both logins succeed reporting the **same** `session_state`.
The restart 302 sets no cookie at all when the browser still has a live
authentication session, and two when it does not.

### The verbs, and the sharpest F31 data point yet

```
GET      200, and it attempts the login - see above
POST     the families above
PUT      405 {"error":"HTTP 405 Method Not Allowed"}
DELETE   405, the same
PATCH    405, the same
OPTIONS  200, Allow: HEAD, POST, GET, OPTIONS
HEAD     404
```

`/auth` answers `HEAD` with **200**. The two endpoints are in one flow on one
container, agree on four verbs and disagree on the fifth.

### The page family's third `Cache-Control`

```
GET  /auth                        400 and 403 pages   no Cache-Control at all
GET  /logout                      400 page            no-cache
POST /login-actions/authenticate  400 pages           no-store, must-revalidate, max-age=0
```

One page, three endpoints, three answers, measured side by side.

## Protocol mappers

Measured 2026-08-30. The `Protocol Mappers` tag is 21 operations in three
families of seven - `/client-scopes/{id}/...`, `/client-templates/{id}/...` and
`/clients/{uuid}/...` - and the `client-templates` seven are byte-identical to
their `client-scopes` siblings on all seven, headers included, with the one
exception the parent family has: `POST` echoes its own path in `Location`.

### `PUT .../models/{id}` writes what the body names and two fields of it

A PUT addressed to mapper A carrying mapper B's `id` answers **204 and changes
B**. The path segment only decides whether the request is a 404 at all.

And it writes `protocolMapper` and `config` only, replacing the config outright
rather than merging. `name`, `protocol` and `consentRequired` are read and
discarded: a PUT renaming a mapper answers 204 and does not rename it, and one
moving a mapper to `saml` leaves its protocol alone.

Both are `ClientScopeAdapter`'s own behaviour, reproduced.

### `config` key order is a sized Java map

A mapper's `config` is a Java `HashMap` **sized to its entry count**, which is a
different table size from the one `internal/javamap` models: `javamap.KeyOrder`
gets six of fourteen measured vectors wrong. The conformance cases sidestep it
by using config key sets measured to be order-stable, so their goldens assert
real config bytes with no `UnorderedKeys` mask.

The `audience resolve` mapper on the `roles` scope serves
`{"introspection.token.claim":"true","access.token.claim":"true"}` - not
alphabetical, and reproduced exactly.

### One mapper serialises two ways

`account-console`'s `audience resolve` is `"config":{}` from `GET /clients` and
populated from the dedicated mapper route, on one container minutes apart. A
client **scope**'s copy is populated in both of its views. Neither "the route
decides" nor "the owner decides" explains both.

### The parse-failure code follows the body's shape, not the endpoint

Corrected 2026-08-30. An earlier section asserted the code was per endpoint and
explained its own error; all eleven of its probes sent `{`, which is the right
shape for the one object endpoint and the wrong shape for the ten array ones.

```
POST /users              with `[`   -> unknown_error
the role-array endpoints with `[`   -> invalid_request
a truncated array element           -> HTTP 400 Bad Request
```

Three codes, decided by the body's first token and by whether the element
parses.

## Scope mappings

Measured 2026-08-30. The tag is 33 operations, eleven each under
`/client-scopes/{id}/scope-mappings/...`, `/client-templates/{id}/...` and
`/clients/{uuid}/...`. **The `client-templates` alias is byte-identical on all
eleven with no exception**, unlike the two tags before it: their one difference
each was `POST` echoing its own path into `Location`, and nothing here mints
one. The alias is distinguishable only through a `Location`.

### The guard is the composite-write rule, not the caller-relative one

Sixteen probe callers. The predicate is **the manage role of the role's own
container** - `manage-realm` for a realm role, `manage-clients` for a client
role - and three measurements falsify reusing the user family's rule:

```
manage-clients                  refused  create-realm, offline_access
                                         (ordinary realm roles, not admin roles;
                                          the caller-relative rule allows both)
manage-clients + manage-realm   ALLOWED  master's `admin`
                                         (the caller-relative rule refuses it:
                                          admin is composite over manage-realm,
                                          not the reverse)
manage-clients                  ALLOWED  master-realm's manage-realm
                                         (the caller-relative rule refuses
                                          exactly this on the user family)
```

It differs from the composite-write rule too: that runs on the add path alone,
this runs on both verbs - a `manage-clients` caller is refused `DELETE` of a
realm role off a scope's mappings where `DELETE .../composites` next door allows
the same removal.

**It is not an escalation surface.** A scope mapping grants nothing; it decides
which of a subject's existing roles survive into a token. The caller-relative
rule exists on the family whose write really does hand out a right.

### The two write pairs read different keys off the same JSON

```
POST|DELETE .../scope-mappings/realm
    resolves each entry by `id`, realm-wide, never looking at `name`
    real id + wrong name   -> 204
    real name + no id      -> 500
    a CLIENT role          -> accepted, and lands under clientMappings

POST|DELETE .../scope-mappings/clients/{client}
    resolves by `name` within that client, ignoring the id
    bogus id + right name  -> 204
    real id + no name      -> 404
```

Four operations, two lookup keys, each ignoring the other's. A decoder that
accepts an entry when *either* matches passes every happy path and gets four
measured rejections wrong.

### `composite` has a third input the other two reads do not

`fullScopeAllowed`. A client carrying it answers **every realm role in the
realm** from `.../scope-mappings/realm/composite` while
`.../scope-mappings/realm` answers `[]` and the combined `.../scope-mappings`
answers `{}`. Turn it off and the composite answers `[]`. A client **scope** has
no such flag. Two of the six bootstrapped clients carry it.

**A client's own roles are in its own scope without ever being mapped**:
`GET /clients/{c}/scope-mappings/clients/{that same c}/available` is `[]` on a
client that owns roles, has the flag off and has mapped nothing, and the
`composite` beside it answers those roles.

**`available` is the complement of the direct list; `composite` is not its
complement.** With one composite role mapped, its child is in the composite
expansion **and** in the available list, on both triples.

### Smaller measurements

```
briefRepresentation      honoured by .../composite alone; six other reads ignore it
the 204s                 no Cache-Control at all
{client} segment 404     `Could not find client` here, `Client not found` on the
                         role-mapping family for the same missing client
415                      exists, and is reachable here because these are the
                         first routes whose DELETE carries a body
PUT, PATCH               a real 405; POST and DELETE on the five read-only paths
                         answer the 404 - the role-mapping paths' split exactly
```

## The device authorization grant

Measured 2026-08-30. Two endpoints, **twenty-two distinct answers** where the
catalogue named eight - the eight it named all measured the grant being
*disabled*, which is its state on every client of a default 26.7.1.

### `POST /realms/{realm}/protocol/openid-connect/auth/device`

The success carries a `Cache-Control` and the refusals do not. `scope` is **not
validated**: `scope=bogus-scope` is accepted, which the authorization endpoint
refuses with `invalid_scope`.

A repeated parameter here answers **`invalid_grant`** with the description
`duplicated parameter` - the same sentence the authorization endpoint sends
under `invalid_request`, and the token endpoint sends under `invalid_request`
too. Three endpoints, one sentence, two codes.

### `expired_token` is a window, not a state

A device code past its expiry keeps answering `expired_token` for a while and
then answers `Device code not valid`, which is what a code that never existed
gets. Bracketed at three client lifespans:

```
lifespan   last expired_token   first "not valid"
       1    mint+16s (+15s)      mint+17s (+16s)
       5    mint+18s (+13s)      mint+21s (+16s)
      20    mint+30s (+10s)      mint+35s (+15s)
```

Fifteen seconds sits inside every bracket and no formula fits all three more
tightly; the boundary reproduced exactly across two runs at one-second
granularity for lifespan 1. **No mechanism for the number has been found**, so
it is a measured approximation rather than an understanding.

Both extremes an implementation reaches for are wrong. Sweeping at expiry
answers `Device code not valid` where Keycloak answers `expired_token`. Never
sweeping answers `expired_token` for ever, which is a leak as well as a
divergence.

### The poll clock is stamped by some answers and not others

A wrong-client poll and a `slow_down` leave it alone; pending and denied move
it. Polls at t=0, 3 and 6 with an interval of 5 give pending, `slow_down`,
pending.

**A denied device code is not consumed and a redeemed one is.**

The access token's `jti` carries the prefix **`onrtdg:`**, which is a fifth
alongside `onrtac:`, `onrtro:`, `onrtrt:` and `onltac:`.

### One condition, two endpoints, two families disagreeing in opposite ways

The device grant's "grant disabled" pair share neither code nor sentence.
CIBA's share both and differ on the **status**: 401 against 400.

## CIBA, and why it cannot complete here

`POST /realms/{realm}/protocol/openid-connect/ext/ciba/auth`.

**`login_hint` is checked before `scope`**, and presence and value are four
interleaved checks rather than two. Only a request missing **both** parameters
reveals the order - which is why a case set that breaks one parameter at a time
passes a wrong implementation, and did.

A repeated parameter is **not** checked here at all.

**A valid request from a CIBA-enabled client answers 503 `server_error` /
"Failed to send authentication request"**, because the default
`ciba-http-auth-channel` needs an external endpoint that `start-dev` does not
configure. The container log says `Authentication Channel Request URI not set
properly`.

So no default container mints an `auth_req_id`, and the two polling cases are
**unmeasurable in this project's container regime** rather than unimplemented.
That is a different thing from "not built", and their `Reason` says which.

## A user create's inline `credentials`

Measured 2026-08-30, after `POST /users` was found ignoring the array.

```
POST /users {"username":"x","credentials":[{"type":"password","value":"p"}]}
    201, and the user can use the password grant immediately
PUT /users/{id} with the same field
    honoured too - the same field, a second route
an entry with no value (or null)
    500 on the create, 400 {"errorMessage":"Could not update user!"} on the
    update, and the whole request rolls back
{"type":"password","value":""}
    201, storing a credential with no credentialData at all
```

The third row is a third answer to a missing password, after `reset-password`'s
`No password provided`. The fourth is Keycloak's own defect.

**It is not `reset-password`'s code path**, and two measurements say so.
`userLabel` is never read here, where `reset-password` *clears* it. And
**`temporary` is a disjunction over the array that only ever adds**
`UPDATE_PASSWORD`: both orderings of `{true, false}` leave the action on, and a
non-temporary entry on a user that already carries it leaves it there - where
`reset-password` with `temporary: false` removes it.

**Each entry replaces the one before it.** An array of two passwords leaves one
credential holding the second value: the first no longer grants, and the listing
has one row.

## A protocol mapper id collides three ways across five routes

Corrected 2026-08-30, twice - the first correction was itself wrong.

The id is unique across the **server**, not the realm: a client scope created in
a *different* realm carrying an id already in use in master is refused. Five
routes enforce it, not three. And the two 409 messages are not chosen by which
route asked:

```
same container,  POST .../protocol-mappers/models      Protocol mapper exists with same name
same container,  POST .../protocol-mappers/add-models  Duplicate resource error
other container, POST .../protocol-mappers/models      Duplicate resource error
other container, POST .../protocol-mappers/add-models  Duplicate resource error
```

So the **location** decides on `models` and decides nothing on `add-models`.
And `PUT /clients/{uuid}` is not a 409 at all - it answers **400
`invalid_input`** - which makes three answers across the five routes.

Getting that fifth route right needed one more thing nobody had written down:
**`PUT /clients/{uuid}` matches its protocol mappers by (protocol, name), not by
id.** Without it every read-modify-write through that route becomes a 400.

## SSO, consent, and the device grant's browser half

Measured 2026-08-30, completing the browser flow.

### A signed-in browser gets a code without a form

A second `GET /auth` from a browser holding `KEYCLOAK_IDENTITY` is a **302 with
a real code**, not the login page. The **original** user session id is carried
into a fresh `AUTH_SESSION_ID`, the original `auth_time` survives into the new
token, and the first session is still refreshable afterwards.

### `prompt` is a set of tokens

Space-separated, case-sensitive, and **an unknown value is ignored**. `create`
fires only as the sole token.

```
prompt=none     with a session   302 with a code
                without one      302 login_required
prompt=login    with a session   re-authentication
prompt=consent  with a session   the consent page, and the user session survives it
prompt=create   registration; its 400 page carries a Cache-Control
prompt=zzz      ignored
```

**`/auth`'s page family disagrees with itself about `Cache-Control`.**
`prompt=create`'s 400 page sends `no-store, must-revalidate, max-age=0`;
`max_age=abc`'s 400 page beside it sends none. So the variable is the rejection,
not the endpoint.

### A response can set one cookie twice, in opposite directions

A presented `KC_RESTART` is cleared on the way out, and **the clear is last**,
so a browser arriving with one leaves without it.

This was found by **recording a golden rather than by probing**. `curl` drops a
`Max-Age=0` cookie from its jar and the harness keeps it as an empty value, so
every hand probe showed five cookies and the recorder showed six. When a probe
and a golden disagree about a cookie count, the golden is the one that saw
everything.

### The device verification page cannot be submitted

`/realms/{realm}/device` and `/protocol/openid-connect/auth/device` are **one
endpoint at two paths**. So the theme's own verification form posts
`device_user_code` into the *device authorization request*, and gets

```
401 {"error":"invalid_client", ...}
```

Six probes. **A user can only complete a device login through
`verification_uri_complete`.** The form is the contract, broken as it is.

### The consent endpoint reads `cancel` and nothing else

```
cancel alone             denied
accept and cancel both   denied - cancel wins
accept alone             approved
neither                  approved
code=BOGUS with accept   approved, on a consent that had just been revoked
```

The hidden `code` is not checked. Requiring `accept` is the obvious
implementation and it is wrong on two of the six measured bodies.

**The device grant asks for consent every time**, on a client that does not
require it, and records a grant it then ignores. The browser flow remembers one.

## Required actions, and the authentication SPI registry

Measured 2026-08-30.

### Five refusals for one alias, and two of them differ only in a full stop

```
GET    /required-actions/{alias}                 404  Failed to find required action
PUT    /required-actions/{alias}                 404  Failed to find required action
DELETE /required-actions/{alias}                 404  Failed to find required action.
POST   /required-actions/{alias}/raise-priority  404  Failed to find required action.
POST   /required-actions/{alias}/lower-priority  404  Failed to find required action.
```

One resource, one key, two sentences split by **verb**. That is the
`Realm not found.` pattern a third time.

### `/config` resolves the alias as a provider id, not as a row

The finding the obvious implementation gets backwards, and it took a rename to
see it. Rename `CONFIGURE_TOTP`'s row to `ZZZ`:

```
ZZZ/config                          400  RequiredAction is not configurable
ZZZ/config-description              404  Could not find configurable RequiredAction provider
CONFIGURE_TOTP/config               404  Could not find RequiredAction config
CONFIGURE_TOTP/config-description   200  - for a row that no longer exists
```

So the sub-resource is keyed on the **provider**, and the row it describes may
have been renamed out from under it.

### `PUT {}` orphans a required action

204, renamed to the empty string, unreachable by alias, and still in the listing
with no `alias` key at all. `alias` and `name` on that body have **opposite**
emptiness rules.

### Other measurements

- **The first strict JSON decoder in this API**, on two of the tag's three
  writes; the third is lax. An unrecognised field is
  `Invalid json representation for RequiredActionProviderRepresentation.
  Unrecognized field "bogusField" at line 1 column 123.`
- **A client authenticator's two property lists are different lists**
  (`federated-jwt`). Four of the five agree, so an implementation with a single
  list passes four of five.
- **A created realm has two authentication executions master has not got.** The
  client scopes' "identical in every realm" does not extend to flows.
- `per-client-config-description` is a JSON object keyed by client-authenticator
  id, not a list - the one of the five provider endpoints that is a map.
